Against that, what scored: on the 2026-09-05 three-scorer Clique run
Snapback 2 beat Convex on authorization (82 vs 79) and offline (81 vs 44)
and lost on queries and live (60 vs 85), client integration (59 vs 79),
setup and docs (55 vs 79), diagnostics and loop (66 vs 77), operations (53
vs 76) and scale (62 vs 80); overall 66 vs 81. The later single-lane 0.0.21
and 0.0.25 scores of 82 and 85 are real improvement and not a three-scorer
comparison. The wins are the core; the losses are composition, discovery,
scale and operations — the substrates and what sits on them.

LLP 2000 §2 summarises this as "costs arise where one layer must reconstruct
another layer's facts." True, and the layer doing the reconstructing is the
client, because it was designed as a cache that may not compute. Name the
cause and the remedy follows.

## 3. Where LLP 2000 is right, and should not be diluted

- **The whole application is the unit.** §3's four properties (composition,
  locality, continuity, performance) are the right acceptance vocabulary.
  Keep them as the headings of the scenario suite.
- **Ownership before abstraction.** §5's table is the best paragraph in the
  document. §8 below turns its fourth row into a fixed vocabulary.
- **Composed reads must not fabricate.** Unknown totals stay unknown; revoked
  rows do not linger. Both flips below preserve this; they change what is
  *known*, not what is *claimed*.
- **Writes have one lifecycle with durable admission distinct from commit
  and stable operation identity.** Keep LLP 1018.004's three outcomes.
- **Rust and SQLite.** Yes, and the engine's transaction, interpreter, rule
  evaluation and successor logic are the pieces to copy first (§5).
- **Prototype the experience before the core.** Yes — and §8 makes the
  prototype's API signatures binding on the implementation, not the reverse.
- **Deterministic simulation early**, citing TigerBeetle. This is the single
  most important sentence in LLP 2000 and §11 makes it the primary harness.
- **A real deployment and a native client early.** Yes; §11 puts the native
  client in slice one.
- **Parallel, fenced, 2000-series, promotion is the author's later call.** Yes.

## 4. Where LLP 2000 is wrong or leaves the wrong things open

### 4.1 It reopens the language and leaves the bound model open; invert that

§6 asks to "evaluate Quarry" and "evaluate any alternative against the same
contracts." The one-language decision took thirty-seven paper applications,
a three-family panel, a spelling spike and an author ruling (LLP
0329.014.004 §9, LLP 1004, LLP 1007 §4b) to close, and the shipped result is
the most readable artifact in Snapback 2: the chat schema is 129 lines and a
query is three to six. Reopening the language will consume the first month.
What *should* be reopened is the bound model under it, which §6 phrases as a
comparison to "concentrate early investigation on." Half of the Quarry
refusals a builder meets are the certifier's byte and step prices wearing
Quarry syntax; the `fn`/`proc` split and the page-to-list gap (LLP 1018.008
F4) are bounded-composition restrictions the pricing model forced. Decide
the bound model (§6), keep the language, and re-derive its restrictions.

### 4.2 The first slice is at the scale where Snapback 2 looked elegant

§10 proposes "a private conversation with paginated messages and an unread
count," "two principals and two clients." Snapback 2 passed that slice in
its first week. It broke at the hub: 1,000 memberships refused the digest
fold, made global search cost a thousand requests, and refused the
envelopes builders first wrote (LLPs 1023, 1024, 1025). Every one of those
was discovered by a cold builder on a published package weeks after the
architecture was fixed. The first slice must include the hub principal from
day one (§11).

### 4.3 It investigates the client data model; the field has answered

§6 says "test a canonical local store with deliberate derived indexes,
durable pending operations, and explicit coverage and authorization
knowledge." That sentence describes a replica; the field has shipped it
under several names (buckets, mutators and a change stream, per-device
queues), Snapback 2's own LLP 1012 §6 reaches it by the hard road ("the
coverage rule that makes everything on the phone exact" is a fully drained
partition), and LLP 1019's `deliver` already computes the audience the
engine must maintain. Decide it (§7) and spend the investigation budget on
the two things the field has *not* settled for this design: partition
inversion of Quarry rules, and horizons at hub scale.

### 4.4 "Builder, human or agent" is two audiences

§3's first row conflates them. Agents need structured refusals with a

### 3.7 The member protocol

K0a's review (2026-10-21; Sol and Grok, blind) found that the K0a seam
routes every producer but cannot yet batch or queue them. Producers still
own the physical transaction: a member's `tx.rollback()` or an early return
that drops its `Tx` would undo earlier members in a shared batch. Hooks run
inside the body, and several of them are the member's result (a job's
status read, the expiry sweep's continuation). The protocol the queue and
the serial batch need:

- **Member-scoped transaction.** A member receives a facade over the batch
  transaction, opened as `SAVEPOINT member`. Its `complete` releases the
  savepoint; `rollback`, `abort` and active-facade drop roll back to it. Only the
  committer issues `BEGIN` and the durable `COMMIT`. Nested savepoints
  (`program`, `job_chunk`, `timeline_chunk`, `seed_sequences`) stay valid
  inside it. Administrative members that need a raw opener (activation and
  the Following launch share one `IMMEDIATE` transaction) remain solo
  members, alone in their batch (§3.2).
- **Explicit disposition.** A member ends as **complete** (its writes stay)
  or **abort** (rolled back to its savepoint), never by inference from
  `Result::Ok`. Paths that return `Ok` without committing today, such as a
  replay rollback or a validation refusal, are aborts. Work they did before
  deciding, such as the writer-time pin, must not be persisted by a later
  member's commit.
- **Continuations, not hooks.** A member returns a **pending result**: owned
  continuation data plus what the caller is told once it runs. After the
  batch's durable `COMMIT`, the committer runs each member's continuation
  **in revision order** and only then acknowledges that member. Continuations
  include process-state changes that must never run ahead of the database:
  catalog and identity replacement, `accept_open`, ephemeral rebind,
  `expiry_after` clearing, scheduler rotation, the entropy advance, and the
  `committed` wakeup. A continuation that needs another durable write, such
  as the sweep's follow-on publication, **submits a new member**. It never
  opens a transaction itself. Caller-facing reads that a hook performs
  today, such as a job's status, run in the continuation after `COMMIT`.
  A continuation's failure is reported separately from the member's
  durable outcome. The write has committed, and the caller must be able to
  tell the two apart.
- **Owned commands against a worker-owned engine.** Member bodies today
  capture the caller's `&mut Engine`, so they cannot cross a queue. Members
  become owned commands (operation, arguments, principal, admission facts)
  that the committer thread executes against the engine it owns; worker
  state is supplied at execution, not captured. Request threads submit and
  wait for the acknowledgement, and `serve` stops holding `FairMutex<Engine>`
  around durable writes (§3.1).
- **A failed batch** rolls back every member's writes and runs no
  continuation. Each member reruns alone, as §3.4 specifies.

K0's remaining slices take this in order: the member-scoped transaction and
disposition (behavior-neutral with single-member batches), then
continuations, then owned commands and the queue, then the direct
operations named by K0a's census, then publication members, then the serial
batch. The census allowlist and the whole suite gate each slice.

## 9. Increments

| Increment | Delivers | Evidence |
| --- | --- | --- |
| K0. Seam and stream | The `Committer` trait. Every producer routed through one stream, executing in place, with single-member batches for unkeyed kinds and the serial batch for keyed ones. `serve` submits to a queue. Per-member post-commit hooks. Publication members replace request-thread `advance_time_audiences`. This is P0's S9 slice, expanded | The whole suite unchanged; the serial batch against the reference on the store oracles; the single-connection SQLite cell before and after |
| K1. Read recording and transcripts | The read set recorded at the boundary, with admission facts read through the recorder; input transcripts (ids, presence, chunk decisions, effective time) | Recording completeness by the site census (child: read sets §6.1); the replay oracle passing over in-place execution for every transcribed kind |
| K2. Overlay | The write overlay, qualified per family, with charge schedules and transition effects (child: overlay) | The overlay differential per family |
| K3. Validation and pre-execution on SQLite | Execution on readers, validation, placeholders, durable allocations, time boundaries, the conflict budget, adaptive batches | The replay oracle and the validation adversary on SQLite; the SQLite and schema-contention cells |
| K4. Postgres | The same on the Postgres adapter | The replay oracle on Postgres; child C's cells on the Hetzner pair |
| K5. Default on | Pre-execution on by default for eligible members | K3–K4 green; the sizing rule cleared on measured numbers |
| K6. Decomposition | Engine members one by one: their framing through `StoreTx`, their programs pre-executable (job chunks, timeline delivery, effect claim and settle) | Per member kind: transcript, oracle, differential |

### K0a implementation note

K0a introduces the crate-private reference `Committer`, `Member` and `Kind`
in `snapback4-core/src/engine/committer.rs`. Each member runs in place and
opens its original transaction through the committer. Its body selects the
original commit point; explicit rollback and implicit drop remain distinct.
Post-commit continuations run synchronously in submission order. A
`Committed<T>` acknowledgement carries hooks across the adapter/engine
boundary for activation and reset, including ephemeral rebind and expiry
continuation clearing. There is no queue, batching, retry or persisted revision.

The sequenced producers in §2, including trusted seed deletion, now enter
that seam. Activation rebuild and Following launch retain their shared
activation transaction; `seed_sequences` remains a savepoint in seed's
member. The private time-clock pin also enters as a direct member. Mutation's
publication prelude stays in place; the evaluation Postgres `mutate_once`
path is unchanged. Host wakeups remain at their existing caller boundaries.

`crates/snapback4/tests/committer_ratchet.rs` shares the store-port ratchet's
lexer and permits only falling production opener counts outside the committer
and adapters. The base has 62 matching lines; K0a leaves 49 in 11 files.
The remaining K0b candidates cover identity/session operations, action/route
admission and lifecycle, work claims/settlement/leases/presence, action/effect
steps, asset intents/views/sweeps/receipt-only reports, job/schedule admission,
opening upgrades and development replay schedule metadata. Six of the 49
are authority snapshots conservatively counted because their handle can
write. Adapter-internal transactions are excluded, so their typed identity,
release and maintenance entry points also need K0b review. This is the first
slice of K0, not completion of the stream increment.

K0 and K1 are useful whatever S1a says. K1's read sets also feed increment
E (LLP 3000.009 §7.1).

**S1a's verdict (2026-10-20).** The predicted ceiling at 0.5 ms is 4,775
commits/s, bound by the execution stage at 32 connections (about 12 round
trips per execution). That is about 40× today's commit path, but 13.5% of
raw Postgres with 32 writers against the 25% × 2 the pre-registered rule
requires. So **K2–K5 wait**. First comes the rule's named fallback:

- reduce the round trips per execution (prefetch and batched reads on the
  execution path, which also speed the in-place path);
- or raise the connection budget.

The store is then re-sized on the Hetzner pair. K0 and K1 proceed.

**S1b's re-size (2026-10-20): the rule clears, and K2–K5 proceed.** The pair
ran over a real network (RTT p50 0.58 ms) with a separate 12-thread
database host:

- Batching same-level reads and folding setup into the first flight cut
  execution to 2 round trips: 9,544/s at C = 32.
- The full-write-set commit stage reaches 4,256/s within the SLO.
- Raw Postgres on that host reaches about 5,900/s at C = 32.

So the predicted 4,256/s clears the required 2,945/s by 45%. Two
consequences follow:

- **Round-trip reduction becomes part of K3/K4.** It also speeds in-place
  members.
- **The single committer is a structural ceiling.** Raw Postgres scales
  past it at C ≥ 64, and the ceiling is recorded in child C's ledger as a
  design-choice limit. On the 96-core host behind the proxy (S1a), the
  same rule failed, because raw Postgres there was six times faster. The
  25%-of-raw bar depends on the hardware, and the absolute per-store commit
  rate is what child C reports.

### K0b implementation note

`Committer::run(store, Member::new(kind, body))` owns the original fenced
`Store::transaction()` (`BEGIN IMMEDIATE`). The body receives a borrowing
`MemberTx` facade under `SAVEPOINT member`, with the existing `Tx` surface
available through dereference. `commit(value)` releases the savepoint and
returns `Complete(value)`; explicit `rollback(value)` rolls back that frame
and returns `Abort(value)`. `abort(value)` selects a normal abort, and the
facade's drop rolls back the frame. Errors also abort. The committer alone
physically commits a Complete result; Abort/error drops the owning transaction
and issues its original rollback. `Result::Ok` never selects durability.
Nested program/chunk/sequence savepoints retain their original meaning.

Mutation replay, input/asset validation refusals, no queued job, no pending
timeline work and empty row-view scans abort. A program refusal still rolls
back only `program`, records its receipt and completes. Job/timeline refusal
parking, clock-only publication and stale/never-live opening cleanup complete,
including their original private metadata. Seed/load/restore, deletion,
expiry/orphan repair, reset and the smoke barrier complete at their original
commit points. Every aborted writer-time pin and metadata write is discarded.
Activation and Following retain `run_solo` / `Solo::open_solo_with`, explicitly
one administrative member with the original raw immediate transaction.

Job status reads, expiry continuation and follow-on audience advancement,
timeline rotation, and mutation's entropy assignment now run after `run`
returns from physical COMMIT. Reset's `run_committed` acknowledgement and
activation's solo acknowledgement retain all existing adapter/engine hooks,
including catalog/identity acceptance, narration, ephemeral rebind and expiry
continuation clearing. A sweep follow-on can still open its own transaction;
a post-commit error still reports after the sweep has committed.

The facade tests execute two members inside one physical transaction: the
first completes, and the second explicitly rolls back, drops or errors. The
first survives the final COMMIT; the second's writes, clock pin and metadata do
not. They also cover every nested frame name, lazy sequence-table rollback,
normal Abort versus Ok, a follow-on writer after COMMIT, and a deferred
constraint failure at physical COMMIT with no acknowledgement or hook.
There is no production batching, continuation protocol, queue or retry yet;
the remaining opener census and host metadata surface are unchanged.

### K0c implementation note

`MemberTx` exposes `Rows` and narrow typed methods for receipts, commit
records, publication, job chunks, effects, seed/reset and asset placement.
It exposes no owning `Tx`, dereference, raw connection, `abandon` or physical
transaction control. Nested savepoint names are restricted to `program`,
`job_chunk`, `timeline_chunk` and `seed_sequences`; neither the member frame
nor SQL fragments can be supplied through that surface.

`complete`, explicit `rollback` and normal `abort` consume the facade and
return an opaque disposition. Drop rolls back an active frame. The driver
checks its final frame state against the token before physical COMMIT, including
rollback failures. Both explicit `rollback` and normal `abort` propagate the
original rollback error with its rusqlite cause; no acknowledgement or
continuation runs and the owning physical transaction drops. K0b ignored
normal-abort rollback errors and returned the member value, but a failed
savepoint rollback cannot prove the isolation needed to acknowledge an abort
in a shared batch. K0c therefore uses the original error rather than `Ok` or
a cause-less disposition contradiction. Test-only injection of `SQLITE_BUSY`
and `SQLITE_IOERR` pins the cause, refusal/retry policy, busy classification
and discarded writes against explicit rollback. Tests forge both contradictory
dispositions inside the committer's private test module and prove they refuse before durability.
Type-checked negative surface assertions reject an owning dereference,
`AsRef<Tx>`/`Borrow<Tx>`, connection access, abandon and physical commit.

Members now return `Pending<T, D>`: an owned caller value and owned
continuation data. `Member::pending` adapts existing body results before the
physical transaction ends. `run_pending` resolves Complete/Abort only after
COMMIT/rollback, and `Committer::run_with` runs the continuation before returning
the caller value. Abort returns its existing value without running a continuation.
Plain `run` supplies an empty continuation. No transaction borrow can survive
into follow-up work. Production remains single-member; there is no queue or retry.

| Previous post-commit work | Pending data and committer continuation |
| --- | --- |
| Quarry/native job status read | Optional owned job id; `run_job_chunk_for` reads `job` after COMMIT |
| Sweep cursor and follow-on publication | Owned expiry cursor map and clock; `sweep_expired_for` installs the map, then `advance_time_audience` submits a new Publication member through the committer |
| Timeline rotation | Rotation flag, index and count; `timeline_chunk` advances the scheduler only for completed work |
| Mutation entropy | Optional entropy; `mutate` installs it only for a completed member |
| Activation store acceptance | Owned report, phase, catalog, identity, announcement flag and ephemeral table names/prefixes computed before COMMIT; solo continuation installs the catalog/identity and calls `accept_open` |
| Activation narration and engine acceptance | Owned population facts, schema/programs and opening metadata; acknowledged continuation stages update narration, schema/generation, scheduler, expiry cursor and ephemeral binding |
| Development reset | Owned `ResetState`; continuation clears expiry, resets the scheduler, rebinds ephemeral state and constructs the asset-sweep reply |
| Host durable wake | Owned head/identity/generation and existing wake selection; `continue_durable_publication` runs the host's wake-state update and both notifications at the original caller boundary |

The solo adapter/engine stages retain a `Committed<T>` acknowledgement between
them. Its only transformation attaches owned data for another committer-run
continuation; the old `after_commit` hook surface is gone. Host publication reads
an already durable head and accepted engine identity while its existing guard is
held; its wake conditions and notification order are unchanged.

The job status, sweep cursor/publication, timeline rotation, mutation entropy,
store acceptance (including the ephemeral filter), narration, adoption/release
and reset continuations now capture no caller borrows: their payloads are
owned and the driver supplies the store/engine context separately. Host durable
publication still captures `&Shared` in `serve_ephemeral::publish_durable`;
that borrow and conversion from inline callbacks to queued commands remain for
the queue slice. No `Send + 'static` callback contract is imposed here.

`RuntimeError::Continuation(source)` means the write committed and its follow-up
failed. Body/open/COMMIT failures retain their original error variants. The
wrapper delegates Display, code, family, refusal, retryability and busy policy to
the source, retaining existing caller behavior while Rust callers can distinguish
the durable outcome. A failed continuation never rolls back the acknowledged write.
`underlying()` strips any nested continuation wrappers for variant classification
without consuming the durable-outcome marker. The error-pattern audit changed
`serve_adoption::retryable`'s Generation test to use it, with a wrapped-error
regression. Job status and sweep-publication consumers in maintenance, the job
HTTP door, CLI and differential runners propagate errors or use code/refusal/
Display/busy methods; none has a reachable raw variant test to change. Runner
budget/refusal tests operate inside member bodies and see unwrapped errors.
Activation-cache's raw Sql/OutOfMemory match and adoption's raw Generation
reopen arm intentionally remain limited to pre-commit errors: a continuation
failure must not replay an already committed activation.

The test-only batch driver uses the same frame execution and continuation
runner as production: two members share one physical transaction, then their
continuations run in submission order. A failed first continuation retains
both durable writes and does not suppress the second continuation. A deferred
constraint failure at physical COMMIT runs neither continuation. Other witnesses
cover abort without follow-up, follow-on submission reading the first member's
committed state, and the original error/refusal policy on follow-up failure.
The member isolation witness now preserves member 1's nonzero sequence counter
across member 2's rollback, drop or error; recorder reset starts from explicitly
unknown evidence, and every nested name still exercises lazy counter creation.

The per-member SQLite cost is inherently SAVEPOINT + RELEASE, two statements
on success (K0b Sol P2 #3 accepted). A later measured optimization may skip the
savepoint for a lone member; this slice retains it.

### K0d (S10d-1) implementation note

   125	    pub(crate) fn rollback_to(&self, name: &str) -> Result<(), RuntimeError> {
   126	        self.tx.rollback_to(Self::nested(name)?)
   127	    }
   128	}

   135	/// Owned follow-up data and the value acknowledged after it runs.

   151	/// A durable acknowledgement may cross adapter/engine continuation stages.
   152	pub(crate) struct Committed<T>(T);
   153	impl<T> Committed<T> {
   154	    pub(crate) fn pending<D>(self, data: D) -> Resolution<T, D> {

   156	            committed: true,
   157	            pending: Pending::new(self.0, data),

   165	/// Constructed only by the physical driver, before any continuation runs.

   170	
   171	/// Scoped openers preserve legacy identity transaction modes without exposing
   172	/// an owning transaction to member bodies. Only the driver receives the handle.
   173	pub(crate) trait TransactionSource {
   174	    fn with_transaction<T>(self, run: impl FnOnce(AnyTx<'_>) -> Result<T, RuntimeError>) -> Result<T, RuntimeError>;
   175	}
   176	impl TransactionSource for &mut AnyStore {
   177	    fn with_transaction<T>(self, run: impl FnOnce(AnyTx<'_>) -> Result<T, RuntimeError>) -> Result<T, RuntimeError> {
   178	        run(self.transaction()?)
   179	    }
   180	}
   181	
   182	pub(crate) struct Committer;
   183	impl Committer {
   184	    pub(crate) fn run<T>(
   185	        store: impl TransactionSource,
   186	        member: Member<impl FnOnce(MemberTx<'_, '_>) -> MemberResult<T>>,
   187	    ) -> Result<T, RuntimeError> {
   188	        store.with_transaction(|tx| Self::run_transaction_value(tx, member))
   189	    }
   190	
   191	    pub(crate) fn run_pending<T, D>(
   192	        store: &mut AnyStore,
   193	        member: Member<impl FnOnce(MemberTx<'_, '_>) -> MemberResult<Pending<T, D>>>,
   194	    ) -> Result<Resolution<T, D>, RuntimeError> {
   195	        Self::run_transaction(store.transaction()?, member)
   196	    }
   197	
   198	    // @ref LLP 3000.009.001.000.000#3.7 — the driver owns BEGIN and COMMIT, including request-planned transactions.
   199	    pub(crate) fn run_request_pending<T, D>(
   200	        store: &mut AnyStore,
   201	        request: Option<(&str, &str, Option<&str>)>,
   202	        member: Member<impl FnOnce(MemberTx<'_, '_>) -> MemberResult<Pending<T, D>>>,
   203	    ) -> Result<Resolution<T, D>, RuntimeError> {
   204	        match request {
   205	            Some((digest, viewer, op)) => Self::run_transaction(
   206	                store.request_write_transaction(digest, viewer, op)?, member,
   207	            ),
   208	            None => Self::run_pending(store, member),
   209	        }
   210	    }
   211	
   212	    pub(crate) fn run_transaction<T, D>(
   213	        tx: AnyTx<'_>,
   214	        member: Member<impl FnOnce(MemberTx<'_, '_>) -> MemberResult<Pending<T, D>>>,
   215	    ) -> Result<Resolution<T, D>, RuntimeError> {
   216	        let disposition = Self::execute(&tx, member)?;
   217	        let committed = disposition.end == Frame::Complete;
   218	        if committed {
   219	            tx.commit()?;
   220	        } else {
   221	            drop(tx);
   222	        }
   223	        Ok(Resolution {
   224	            committed,
   225	            pending: disposition.value,
   226	        })
   227	    }
   228	
   229	    pub(crate) fn run_transaction_value<T>(
   230	        tx: AnyTx<'_>,
   231	        member: Member<impl FnOnce(MemberTx<'_, '_>) -> MemberResult<T>>,
   232	    ) -> Result<T, RuntimeError> {
   233	        Self::run_transaction(tx, member.pending(Pending::value)).map(|r| r.pending.value)
   234	    }
   235	
   236	    /// Host context is borrowed separately for the body and its continuation;
   237	    /// no transaction borrow can reach the continuation or its follow-on member.
   238	    // @ref LLP 3000.009.001.000.000#3.7 — acknowledge in submission order only after durable COMMIT and continuation.
   239	    pub(crate) fn run_with<H, T, D>(
   240	        host: &mut H,
   241	        prepare: impl FnOnce(&mut H) -> Result<Resolution<T, D>, RuntimeError>,
   242	        continuation: impl FnOnce(&mut H, D, T) -> Result<T, RuntimeError>,
   243	    ) -> Result<T, RuntimeError> {
   244	        let resolution = prepare(host)?;
   245	        Self::finish(host, resolution, continuation)
   246	    }
   247	
   248	    pub(crate) fn run_committed_with<H, T, D, R>(
   249	        host: &mut H,
   250	        prepare: impl FnOnce(&mut H) -> Result<Resolution<T, D>, RuntimeError>,
   251	        continuation: impl FnOnce(&mut H, D, T) -> Result<R, RuntimeError>,
   252	    ) -> Result<Committed<R>, RuntimeError> {
   253	        let resolution = prepare(host)?;
   254	        if !resolution.committed {
   255	            return Err(RuntimeError::Store(
   256	                "commit acknowledgement requires completion".into(),
   257	            ));
   258	        }
   259	        Self::continue_pending(host, resolution.pending, continuation).map(Committed)
   260	    }
   261	
   262	    fn finish<H, T, D>(
   263	        host: &mut H,
   264	        resolution: Resolution<T, D>,
   265	        continuation: impl FnOnce(&mut H, D, T) -> Result<T, RuntimeError>,
   266	    ) -> Result<T, RuntimeError> {
   267	        if resolution.committed {
   268	            Self::continue_pending(host, resolution.pending, continuation)
   269	        } else {
   270	            Ok(resolution.pending.value)
   271	        }
   272	    }
   273	
   274	    fn continue_pending<H, T, D, R>(
   275	        host: &mut H,
   276	        pending: Pending<T, D>,
   277	        continuation: impl FnOnce(&mut H, D, T) -> Result<R, RuntimeError>,
   278	    ) -> Result<R, RuntimeError> {
   279	        let Pending { value, data } = pending;
   280	        continuation(host, data, value)
   281	            .map_err(|source| RuntimeError::Continuation(Box::new(source)))
   282	    }
   283	
   284	    fn execute<T>(
   285	        tx: &AnyTx<'_>,
   286	        member: Member<impl FnOnce(MemberTx<'_, '_>) -> MemberResult<T>>,
   287	    ) -> MemberResult<T> {
   288	        let Member { kind, run } = member;
   289	        // The queue owns the engine; this driver still commits one member,
   290	        // without batching, durable revision allocation or retries.
   291	        let _kind = kind;
   292	        tx.begin_member_evidence();
   293	        tx.savepoint("member")?;
   294	        let frame = Cell::new(Frame::Active);
   295	        let disposition = run(MemberTx {
   296	            tx,
   297	            frame: &frame,
   298	            #[cfg(test)]
   299	            rollback_failure: Cell::new(None),
   300	        })?;
   301	        if !matches!(frame.get(), Frame::Complete | Frame::Abort) || frame.get() != disposition.end
   302	        {
   303	            return Err(RuntimeError::Store(
   304	                "member disposition contradicts its frame".into(),
   305	            ));
   306	        }
   307	        Ok(disposition)
   308	    }
   309	
   310	    /// Test-only batch driver: production still submits exactly one member.
   311	    #[cfg(test)]
   312	    fn run_batch_with<T, D>(
   313	        store: &mut AnyStore,
   314	        members: impl IntoIterator<
   315	            Item = Member<impl FnOnce(MemberTx<'_, '_>) -> MemberResult<Pending<T, D>>>,
   316	        >,
   317	        mut continuation: impl FnMut(&mut AnyStore, D, T) -> Result<T, RuntimeError>,
   318	    ) -> Result<Vec<Result<T, RuntimeError>>, RuntimeError> {
   319	        let tx = store.transaction()?;
   320	        let mut resolutions = Vec::new();
   321	        for member in members {
   322	            let disposition = Self::execute(&tx, member)?;
   323	            resolutions.push(Resolution {
   324	                committed: disposition.end == Frame::Complete,
   325	                pending: disposition.value,
   326	            });
   327	        }
   328	        tx.commit()?;
   329	        // A failed follow-up does not uncommit this or any later member, nor
   330	        // prevent another committed member's continuation from running.
   331	        Ok(resolutions
   332	            .into_iter()
   333	            .map(|r| Self::finish(store, r, &mut continuation))
   334	            .collect())
   335	    }
   336	
   337	    /// Activation and Following prepare one raw IMMEDIATE transaction over a
   338	    /// candidate catalog. This administrative escape is explicitly solo.
   339	    pub(crate) fn run_solo<T, D>(
   340	        kind: Kind,
   341	        run: impl FnOnce(&mut Solo) -> Result<Pending<T, D>, RuntimeError>,
   342	    ) -> Result<Resolution<T, D>, RuntimeError> {
   343	        assert_eq!(
   344	            kind,
   345	            Kind::Administrative,
   346	            "raw transactions are solo administrative members"
   347	        );
   348	        let mut solo = Solo {
   349	            opened: false,
   350	            committed: false,
   351	        };
   352	        let pending = run(&mut solo)?;
   353	        Ok(Resolution {
   354	            committed: solo.committed,
   355	            pending,
   356	        })
   357	    }
   358	}

   360	pub(crate) trait CommitTransaction {
   361	    fn commit_transaction(self) -> Result<(), RuntimeError>;
   362	}
   363	
   364	pub(crate) struct Solo {
   365	    opened: bool,
   366	    committed: bool,
   367	}
   368	impl Solo {
   369	    pub(crate) fn open_solo_with<X>(
   370	        &mut self,
   371	        open: impl FnOnce() -> Result<X, RuntimeError>,
   372	    ) -> Result<X, RuntimeError> {
   373	        assert!(!self.opened, "one transaction per solo member");
   374	        let tx = open()?;
   375	        self.opened = true;
   376	        Ok(tx)
   377	    }
   378	    pub(crate) fn commit(&mut self, tx: impl CommitTransaction) -> Result<(), RuntimeError> {
   379	        assert!(self.opened && !self.committed, "one commit per solo member");
   380	        tx.commit_transaction()?;
     1	//! A mutation's durable result and the facts produced by its commit.
     2	use super::*;
     3	
     4	/// Owned facts for publication and the response, never a historical replay.
     5	// @ref LLP 3000.009.001.000.001#3.4 — carry commit facts only after durable COMMIT.
     6	#[derive(Clone, Debug, PartialEq, Eq)]
     7	pub struct DurablePublication {
     8	    pub store_id: String,
     9	    pub head: u64,
    10	    pub generation: u64,
    11	    pub preserved: Option<Vec<String>>,
    12	}
    13	
    14	#[derive(Debug)]
    15	pub struct MutationAcknowledgement {
    16	    pub outcome: MutationOutcome,
    17	    pub publication: Option<DurablePublication>,
    18	}
    19	impl MutationAcknowledgement {
    20	    fn unpublished(outcome: MutationOutcome) -> Self { Self { outcome, publication: None } }
    21	}
    22	
    23	fn committed_preservation(footprint: crate::revocation::Footprint, schema: &Schema) -> Option<Vec<String>> {
    24	    // Match the persisted evidence reader's bounds and merge normalization.
    25	    // Postgres does not persist footprints yet: its Rows default is unknown.
    26	    if footprint.unknown || crate::value::canonical_json(&footprint).len() > 262144 { return None; }
    27	    let mut normalized = crate::revocation::Footprint::default();
    28	    normalized.merge(&footprint);
    29	    (!normalized.unknown).then(|| normalized.preserved(schema))
    30	}
    31	
    32	impl Engine {
    33	    /// Run a mutation. With an `op` id the write applies at most once: a
    34	    /// replay returns the recorded outcome. The replay check comes before
    35	    /// anything the current generation could refuse (an op renamed, an
    36	    /// argument narrowed), so a write that applied under an earlier schema
    37	    /// is never re-judged: its row is on the server, and the device that
    38	    /// queued it must learn `sent`, not `failed`.
    39	    pub fn mutate(&mut self, name: &str, op: Option<&str>, context: Context) -> Result<MutationOutcome, RuntimeError> {
    40	        self.mutate_inner(name, op, context, false, None, None).map(|ack| ack.outcome)
    41	    }
    42	
    43	    /// Commit-local publication facts, exposed only after physical COMMIT.
    44	    pub fn mutate_acknowledged(&mut self, name: &str, op: Option<&str>, context: Context) -> Result<MutationAcknowledgement, RuntimeError> {
    45	        self.mutate_inner(name, op, context, true, None, None)
    46	    }
    47	
    48	    /// Serving already performed unfenced replay-before-judgment. The final
    49	    /// authority check and serial receipt check belong to this Program member.
    50	    // @ref LLP 3000.009.001.000.001#31-authority-boundaries — a carried credential is not proof of current authority.
    51	    pub fn mutate_authorized(&mut self, name: &str, op: Option<&str>, context: Context,
    52	        digest: &str, sample: impl FnOnce() -> crate::identity::ServiceSample,
    53	        authorize: impl FnOnce(crate::identity::RequestAuthority) -> Result<(), Refusal>,
    54	    ) -> Result<MutationAcknowledgement, Refusal> {
    55	        crate::identity::request::require_digest(digest).map_err(|e| e.refusal())?;
    56	        let mut refusal = None;
    57	        let mut check = Some((sample, authorize));
    58	        let result = self.mutate_inner(name, op, context, true, Some(&mut |tx| {
    59	            let (sample, authorize) = check.take().expect("one authority boundary per in-place member");
    60	            let facts = tx.request_authority(digest, sample())?;
    61	            authorize(facts).map_err(|why| {
    62	                // The RuntimeError unwinds the member frame. Preserve the exact
    63	                // host refusal outside it, rather than converting its envelope.
    64	                let error = RuntimeError::Auth(why.message.clone());
    65	                refusal = Some(why);
    66	                error
    67	            })
    68	        }), Some(digest));
    69	        if let Some(why) = refusal { return Err(why); }
    70	        result.map_err(|error| {
    71	            let mut why = error.refusal();
    72	            // A preflight miss followed by fence loss is still unsent intent.
    73	            if op.is_some() && why.code == "E_STORE_OWNERSHIP" { why.retryable = true; }
    74	            why
    75	        })
    76	    }
    77	
    78	    fn mutate_inner(&mut self, name: &str, op: Option<&str>, mut context: Context, publication_requested: bool, mut authority: Option<&mut dyn FnMut(&super::committer::MemberTx<'_, '_>) -> Result<(), RuntimeError>>, digest: Option<&str>) -> Result<MutationAcknowledgement, RuntimeError> {
    79	        if authority.is_none() { validate::write_identity(&context.viewer, op)?; }
    80	        context.generation = self.generation;
    81	        let requested = context.now.unwrap_or_else(crate::now_ms);
    82	        context.now = Some(self.advance_time_audience(requested)?);
    83	        let wire_args = if op.is_some() { context.wire_args.take().unwrap_or_else(|| {
    84	                serde_json::Value::Object(crate::wire::map_to_json(&context.args))
    85	            }) } else { serde_json::Value::Null };
    86	        if authority.is_none() && let Some(op) = op
    87	            && let Some(outcome) = self.replay_mutation(&context.viewer, op, name, &wire_args)?
    88	        {
    89	            return Ok(MutationAcknowledgement::unpublished(outcome));
    90	        }
    91	        // An existing durable receipt owns its id even after conversion to ephemeral.
    92	        if self.schema.operations.get(name).is_some_and(|f| f.ephemeral) {
    93	            if authority.is_some() { return Err(RuntimeError::Generation("the operation became ephemeral; retry through current admission".into())); }
    94	            self.require_non_media_ephemeral(&context.viewer,op)?;
    95	            self.schema.check_delivery(name, context.delivery_fingerprint.as_deref())?;
    96	            let viewer=context.viewer.clone();
    97	            return match self.mutate_ephemeral(name, context) {
    98	                Ok(out) => Ok(MutationOutcome { receipt_only: false, state: WriteState::Sent { seq: out.seq, result: out.result }, metered: out.metered, tables: out.ephemeral.tables, events: 0, replayed: false }),
    99	                Err(error) if error.is_refusal() => Ok(MutationOutcome { receipt_only: false, state: WriteState::Failed { why: seat_refusal(&self.schema,&error,&viewer), current: None }, metered: Metered::default(), tables: Vec::new(), events: 0, replayed: false }),
   100	                Err(error) => Err(error),
   101	            }.map(MutationAcknowledgement::unpublished);
   102	        }
   103	        let request = digest.map(|digest| (digest, context.viewer.clone(), op));
   104	        Committer::run_with(self, |engine| Committer::run_request_pending(
   105	            &mut engine.store,
   106	            request.as_ref().map(|(digest, viewer, op)| (*digest, viewer.as_str(), *op)),
   107	            Member::new(Kind::Program, |tx| {
   108	            if let Some(check) = authority.as_mut() { check(&tx)?; validate::write_identity(&context.viewer, op)?; }
   109	            // @ref LLP 3000.009.001.000.001#35-each-command-keeps-its-own-place — a later command observes a receipt committed during the head-lock wait.
   110	            if let Some(op) = op
   111	                && let Some(outcome) = tx.replay_bound(&context.viewer, op, name, &wire_args)?
   112	            {
   113	                // Applied between the check above and this transaction.
   114	                let outcome = MutationOutcome::from_receipt(&outcome)?;
   115	                return tx.rollback((MutationAcknowledgement::unpublished(outcome), None));
   116	            }
   117	            if let Some(op)=op{tx.require_placement(&context.viewer,op,name,&wire_args)?;}
   118	            context.now = Some(tx.pin_writer_time(&engine.schema, context.now.unwrap_or(0))?);
   119	            engine.schema.check_delivery(name, context.delivery_fingerprint.as_deref())?;
   120	            let program = engine.programs.get(name).ok_or_else(|| RuntimeError::UnknownOp(name.to_owned()))?.clone();
   121	            if program.kind == ProgramKind::Query {
   122	                return Err(RuntimeError::UnknownOp(format!("{name} is a query, not a mutation")));
   123	            }
   124	            if program.kind == ProgramKind::Action {
   125	                return Err(RuntimeError::UnknownOp(format!("{name} is an action: call it with client.act")));
   126	            }
   127	            if program.kind == ProgramKind::Job {
   128	                return Err(RuntimeError::UnknownOp(format!("{name} is a job: a schedule fires it, or `snapback4 run {name}` does; no caller can")));
   129	            }
   130	            if let Err(error) = validate::validate_inputs(&engine.schema, &program, &mut context.args) {
   131	                return tx.abort((MutationAcknowledgement::unpublished(MutationOutcome { receipt_only: false, state: WriteState::Failed { why: error.refusal(), current: None }, metered: Metered::default(), tables: Vec::new(), events: 0, replayed: false }), None));
   132	            }
   133	            if context.presence.is_none() {
   134	                context.presence = engine.presence.clone();
   135	            }
   136	            context.entropy = engine.entropy;
   137	            context.minter = engine.minter.clone();
   138	            let ceilings = engine.ceilings;
   139	            let viewer = context.viewer.clone();
   140	            let now = context.now.unwrap_or(0);
   141	            // An `image`/`video` argument names an upload by id; the program
   142	            // reads the facts the server established (`clip.duration`), so
   143	            // they are put in place before it runs. An id that is not the
   144	            // writer's own upload stays as sent and the write refuses it.
   145	            for (name, kind) in &program.args {
   146	                let inner = match kind {
   147	                    crate::ir::ArgType::Optional(inner) => inner.as_ref(),
   148	                    other => other,
   149	                };
   150	                if !matches!(inner, crate::ir::ArgType::Image { .. } | crate::ir::ArgType::Video { .. } | crate::ir::ArgType::File { .. }) {
   151	                    continue;
   152	                }
   153	                let Some(id) = asset_id(context.args.get(name)).map(str::to_owned) else { continue;
   154	                };
   155	                if let Some(asset) = tx.asset(&id)?
   156	                    && (asset.owner == viewer || context.is_system())
   157	                {
   158	                    // @ref LLP 3000.038#3 — a file argument never reads an image's facts, nor an image argument a file's.
   159	                    if (asset.kind == AssetKind::File) != matches!(inner, crate::ir::ArgType::File { .. }) {
   160	                        let wanted = if matches!(inner, crate::ir::ArgType::File { .. }) { "a file" } else { "an image or video" };
   161	                        let error = RuntimeError::Asset { code: "E_ASSET_TYPE", message: format!("{name} takes {wanted} and {id} is {}; upload it as {wanted}", asset.kind.word()) };
   162	                        return tx.abort((MutationAcknowledgement::unpublished(MutationOutcome { receipt_only: false, state: WriteState::Failed { why: error.refusal(), current: None }, metered: Metered::default(), tables: Vec::new(), events: 0, replayed: false }), None));
   163	                    }
   164	                    context.args.insert(name.clone(), asset.column_value());
   165	                }
   166	            }
   167	            tx.savepoint("program")?;
   168	            let mut runner = Runner::new(&tx, &engine.schema, context, Meter::new(ceilings, Cut::Abort), tx.next_seq()?);
   169	            #[cfg(feature = "evaluation-support")]
   170	            { runner.meter.used = engine.evaluation_precharge; }
   171	            let ran = runner.run(&program).and_then(|value| {
   172	                runner.charge_output(&value)?;
   173	                Ok(value)
   174	            });
   175	            // Commit-end expiry can cascade or clear rows that an `after insert`
   176	            // witness names. Final obligations therefore inspect the actual
   177	            // provisional commit state, after every retirement action.
   178	            let ran = ran.and_then(|value| runner.retire_expired_births().map(|_| value))
   179	                .and_then(|value| runner.check_final_exists().map(|_| value));
   180	            let metered = runner.meter.used;
   181	            let tables: Vec<String> = runner.written_tables.iter().cloned().collect();
   182	            let events: Vec<Event> = runner.events.take();
   183	            let action_tables = std::mem::take(&mut runner.action_tables);
   184	            let outbox = std::mem::take(&mut runner.outbox);
   185	            let rows_written = runner.rows_written;
   186	            let mut read_tables = runner.read_tables.clone();
   187	            let entropy = runner.context.entropy;
   188	            drop(runner);
   189	            let outcome = match ran {
   190	                Ok(result) => {
   191	                    tx.release("program")?;
   192	                    // @ref LLP 3000.019#4 — P7 reports keep at-most-once custody.
   193	                    // @ref LLP 3000.022#6 — a covered report keeps only a receipt at the committed head.
   194	                    let receipt_only = rows_written == 0 && outbox.is_empty()
   195	                        && engine.schema.delivery.iter().any(|binding| binding.mutation == name);
   196	                    let seq = tx.next_seq()? - u64::from(receipt_only);
   197	                    read_tables.extend(tables.iter().cloned());
   198	                    if !receipt_only {
   199	                        tx.record_commit(seq, now, (name, &viewer, program.kind.word()), &tables, rows_written)?;
   200	                        tx.append(seq, &events)?;
   201	                        tx.record_effects(&outbox, seq, now, engine.dev_effects, &engine.schema)?;
   202	                    }
   203	                    MutationOutcome { receipt_only, state: WriteState::Sent { seq, result }, metered, tables, events: partition::disclosed_event_count(&engine.schema, &events, &action_tables), replayed: false }
   204	                }

### K0d (S10d-1) implementation note

The common engine's admitted identity paths now submit `Kind::Direct` members
through `Committer::run`: guest mint (full/read), ordinary/service/custom open,
password signup and login, full/read refresh, resolve's expired-session pruning,
close, authenticated family logout, principal revocation and read narrowing.
Startup's unconditional stale worker-family cleanup also joins the seam.
The existing bearer authority snapshots remain read-only. Query publication
already uses Publication members; identified mutation success, refusal and
receipt-only outcomes already record their receipts inside the Program member,
including insert-then-bind and replay/id-reuse behavior. They need no second
receipt member.

`TransactionSource` lets the committer receive a scoped owning handle from the
port without lending it to a member. `IdentitySource` retains SQLite's original
unchecked deferred composite openers and immediate read-refresh opener; logout
and narrowing retain the engine's immediate, generation-checked transaction and
sample authority time only after acquiring it. Postgres uses the existing
serialized/head-fenced opener and shared-handle borrow. The restricted facade
adds typed identity facts/edits only; no raw SQL, connection, owning transaction,
transaction callback or physical commit reaches the member. SQL remains inside
the store adapter. No ownership files or `engine/postgres/` files change.

Guest/signup/login, full replacement and revocation keep their original atomic
boundaries. A full replacement that finds no live old row completes its expiry
cleanup; read refresh with absent/expired/unauthorized storage aborts, without
sweeping another family. SQLite's convenience session open and close keep
each formerly autocommitted statement as a separate Direct member, preserving
partial durability on a later statement's failure, including lazy read-session
storage creation before a refused insert. Each unit now has the K0c
member SAVEPOINT/RELEASE around its original write. Plain `run` carries the
owned result through the existing pending-result protocol; none of these paths
has additional post-commit process work to move into a continuation. A failed
physical COMMIT cannot acknowledge a new session. No queue, batching, retry or
new thread is introduced.

The opener census falls from **11 files / 49 sites** to **10 files / 47 sites**:
identity loses logout's opener, and read-session narrowing loses its only
opener. The adapter-internal identity transactions were outside that lexical
census before this slice; routing them is established by the actual shared-port
call paths and tests, rather than by a larger reported census reduction.
Six remaining sites are conservative authority-snapshot matches, not durable
writes. Remaining direct writers outside the committer are outside the
admitted request profile: action/route admission and lifecycle; work/effect
claims, settlement, lease renewal and presence; action/effect steps; asset
intents, views, sweep/placement and asset receipt-only reports; job/schedule
admission; bound action-session mint/recovery; development replay schedule policy.
The administrative opening/upgrade census match is outside this direct-operation
slice and remains part of the parent's public opening/activation prerequisite. The concrete adapters' legacy fixture/evaluator identity
composites, supervised worker mint/revocation and status reports, and development
replay's effect-policy write also remain later K0 work.
Public startup must still gate excluded runners as the parent §3.4 requires.

SQLite regressions cover atomic mint/refresh rollback, convenience mint/close
partial durability, cleanup completion versus read-refresh abort, physical
COMMIT refusal with no returned session, logout's authority-clock boundary,
and absence of public commit/receipt rows from direct operations. Qualification
and the complete remaining-opener inventory are recorded in
[the lane report](../snapback4/reports/20261022-s10d1-direct-members.md).

### K0-Q implementation note

The serving writer now belongs to one named `snapback4-committer` thread.
`engine::command::Command<T>` owns a `Send + 'static` body and receives the
worker's `&mut Engine` at execution. `Queue` carries erased commands on a
bounded FIFO `sync_channel` and returns typed, one-shot acknowledgements.
A queued command cannot borrow the submitting engine or return a transaction
borrow. The same `Engine` and `AnyStore` path serves SQLite and Postgres.
Test-only request counters capture a shared atomic handle at submission and install it for the command body and synchronous continuation, clearing it before the next command so unrelated maintenance and lease renewal stay uncounted.

Serving commands own their operation, arguments, context, principal, ids and
HTTP admission inputs. The admission structures retain credentials and trusted
request facts without retaining sockets or body readers; live session, generation
and authority-clock checks run again against worker state. Password derivation,
HTTP body/response I/O, asset byte I/O and detached query execution remain on
submitting threads. Ephemeral execution uses its existing hub lock and detached
reader, outside the mailbox. Session and receipt checks and reader checkout for
ephemeral requests also use detached readers; a barrier test requires both a
query and an ephemeral mutation to finish before a held durable writer is
released. The in-memory fixture fallback executes on the
worker because that store has no detached connection; production `--memory`
continues to use its private WAL file.

`Shared.engine` is a mailbox handle rather than `FairMutex<Engine>`. Production
has no engine guard to lend a request. Identity writes, mutation preflight and
execution, request-driven publication and jobs, seed/reset, activation, and the
other serving engine accesses run as owned commands. A mutex-backed fixture
adapter exists only under `cfg(test)` for the existing fault/barrier tests.
Detached readers, their epoch replacement, and the ephemeral hub remain separate.
Background turns still defer a short idle request burst, enter the FIFO and
choose the existing contention budget; a continuation's follow-on engine member
executes on the same worker through the existing member driver, without waiting
on its own mailbox.

The mailbox holds **128 waiting commands**, plus the running command. HTTP
submissions and bounded maintenance turns use `try_send`: a full mailbox refuses
before running its body, with the existing HTTP **503 / E_RATE_LIMIT / limit**
refusal and `Retry-After: 1`. Fixed host-control/inspection calls use the same
bounded mailbox and wait at most **100 ms** for space, enough for a short
scheduling burst. Admitted commands wait without an acknowledgement deadline:
a started command delivers its real result, including continuations, however
long COMMIT takes. The former 30-second timeout manufactured unknown outcomes
for successful writes. Lease loss still refuses queued, not-yet-started work
immediately through the independent watchdog. Worker disconnection before
`start_execution` is `Stopped` / `E_COMMITTER_STOPPED`: **not applied**, retryable
after recovery; disconnection after start is `E_COMMIT_OUTCOME_UNKNOWN`, requiring
receipt/key reconciliation. There is no unbounded second command queue.
No accepted write is cancelled when its acknowledgement receiver disappears;
no unknown command is automatically retried.

Both `call` and `submit` refuse a submission made by their own committer worker
before creating an admission ticket or enqueueing: typed `SubmitError::Reentrant`
(`E_COMMITTER_REENTRANT`, HTTP 500, non-retryable/not applied). Command construction
also debug-asserts this invariant after the public admission check. Use the inline
member driver for continuations. The fix-forward production-body audit found no
current same-queue reentry; a real-queue witness covers both doors, an empty
admission registry, subsequent queue progress and a call to a different queue.

Clock-control serialization keeps the 100 ms admission bound and overload
response. Its maintenance pause belongs to the request and clears when that
request ends. The regression holds the writer beyond the former 30-second
deadline, verifies no premature response, then observes one successful advance.
After time advances, a maintenance/report refusal returns **500
E_CLOCK_REPORT_INCOMPLETE**, non-retryable, carrying `advancedMs` and
`reportIncomplete`, with no `Retry-After`. Inspect the clock or drain with
`0ms`; repeating the original relative duration deliberately advances again
because the clock door has no idempotency key. A deterministic mailbox-full
witness exercises this recovery without adding another second.
The request retains the published effective `now` separately from the worker's
acknowledgement. Losing that acknowledgement after publication returns the same
incomplete-report envelope, including `now` and `advancedMs`.

Unknown supervised-child mint outcomes fence the original `worker_revision`,
like failed retirement, before a replacement can be minted. Only overload and
known pre-execution refusals retry at the same revision. Source watchers keep
watching after refusals, retry transient non-admission and never re-adopt an
unknown source digest, including after a stored-generation change; later source
edits remain eligible.
The fence applies to an inner `CommitOutcomeUnknown` inside a successful queue
reply too. An injected post-COMMIT unknown through the actual `DevWorker` tick
and queue leaves a durable orphan that cannot authenticate; both named and
conventional worker credentials have this witness.
An uncertain conventional mint also retains retirement custody: its single
stored family must be revoked before replacement overwrites that marker.
Failed retirement suspends replacement and retains the fence. The witness holds
that retirement behind an external writer, then verifies the orphan stays
unauthorized after the real supervisor starts a replacement. Named families
retain their independent revision markers.

A detached `/q/` reader that encounters `E_GENERATION` is discarded and reopened
behind the adoption command, at most twice. This covers adoption COMMIT before
reader replacement without weakening the generation check: a genuine foreign
activation still refuses. The deterministic witness pauses exactly in that gap
and requires HTTP 200 with the original data and new generation. This race
predates K0-Q; the lifecycle replay retains its 1,600-row/62-edge assertions.
Every attempt decodes the original wire arguments against its executing program,
including the in-memory fallback, so a signature-changing handoff refuses stale
arguments. A query-outcome `E_GENERATION` after both handoffs returns HTTP 503,
matching metadata and reader-open failures. Deterministic adoptions witness both
the changed signature and three consecutive query-outcome generation failures.

The K0b/K0c transaction driver is unchanged: every actual member keeps its own
physical transaction, savepoints, explicit disposition, SQL, receipt and error
policy. Each operation's existing engine continuations run on the worker after
its durable COMMIT, then the worker acknowledges its result. Serving mutation, action, effect, route and maintenance wakes, and memory-sync
publication run inside their original admitted command before acknowledgement;
notification cannot itself be refused as a second mailbox item. Wake conditions
and metadata remain attached to that command's result. Request generation,
development refusal evidence, opt-in trace events, queue/engine wall time and
worker CPU measurements return to the submitting request before encoding.
There is no multi-member batch, fill window, overlay, pre-execution or new durable
revision schema in this slice. The remaining direct-opener census is unchanged.

After rebasing onto S10d-2, a queue can retain the acquired Postgres lease.
The host opens the engine with `open_postgres_owned` and installs renewal
before opening its listener. Renewal runs on its dedicated connection and
thread, outside the mailbox. Shutdown drains accepted work before releasing
the lease. The internal Postgres HTTP witness now follows this positive-token
path; public Postgres configuration and release/startup policy remain S10d-3,
as the parent RFC's S10d-2 implementation note records.

Renewal failure or the independent monotonic lease deadline latches ownership
loss. Queue renewal and public S10d-3 serving share `LeaseDeadline`: measure from
before renewal SQL, expire at the lease period minus min(50 ms, one quarter of
the period), and never revive expired authority on a late renewal completion.
The watchdog never locks the renewal connection; its sleep is at most 10 ms
and shortens to the remaining deadline for short leases. Command admission/start
also check the deadline. Startup retains a release guard until installation
succeeds, including renewal and thread-spawn failures. A renewal that commits
but then fails `renewed()` drops its lease without release, accepting up to one
extra lease period of unavailability rather than reviving expired authority.

`Writer::call` returns a typed `Result`; serving, adoption and worker supervision
handle refusals without panicking. All JSON serving doors use one transport
policy: `E_RATE_LIMIT` → **503**, `Retry-After: 1`, retryable;
`E_STORE_OWNERSHIP` → **409**, not retryable; pre-execution disconnect →
`E_COMMITTER_STOPPED`, **503**, retryable/not applied; worker death after start
or genuine ambiguous COMMIT → `E_COMMIT_OUTCOME_UNKNOWN`, **500**, not retryable. As before,
a held change poll that already emitted HTTP 200 heartbeats reports later errors
in its final typed body. Bulk maintenance returns `Result<bool, RuntimeError>`
so refusal is logged and distinguished from idle work.

A view-once asset consumes its view when a full, non-range read starts (author
ruling, 2026-10-23; LLP 3000 §7). It commits consumption before sending any
response bytes, while retaining per-asset/principal transfer custody. Range
probes refuse with `E_ASSET_RANGE` without consumption; a request known to have
been aborted before admission consumes nothing. Refused or unknown bookkeeping
delivers no asset bytes. The first full GET returns 200 with the full body, and
later full GETs return 410 `E_ASSET_VIEWED`. A failed transport after the commit
still consumes the view, even if no response bytes arrive: one lost view is
accepted to keep bytes from escaping without durable consumption.
Regression witnesses fill the mailbox before delivery and verify a subsequent
fetch is refused, even after the mailbox drains. SQLite file and memory HTTP
witnesses also hold delivery after COMMIT, observe the consumed view before any
response bytes, then verify the complete body and subsequent refusal. A known
aborted pipelined request and range probes leave the view available. The
Postgres witness commits through the same engine method, then reopens the store
and verifies the duplicate refusal and independent principal. Its public HTTP
asset schemas and blob delivery remain excluded under the parent's §3.4 (P1b);
this ledger witness does not claim Postgres HTTP asset support.

After lease loss, new submissions refuse with
`SubmitError::OwnershipLost`, mapped to `E_STORE_OWNERSHIP` by serving.
An admission registry arbitrates command start against lease loss: every
waiting acknowledgement is refused immediately, without waiting for a busy
writer or an uncancellable COMMIT. Queued bodies subsequently drain without
execution. The admission ticket calls this transition `start_execution`, so the
unchanged lexical opener census cannot mistake it for a database `begin`.
Capacity-blocked control callers also observe the loss. A running
command retains its actual result; the unchanged Postgres first-lock token
fence still prevents writes after takeover. Physical COMMIT is never cancelled
or retried, and `CommitOutcomeUnknown` crosses the typed acknowledgement
unchanged. The worker-ledger conflict retains S10d-2's neutral path helper
inside an owned serving command.

Real-Postgres queue witnesses cover sixteen queued mutations and one
capacity-blocked caller refused while the active command remains paused,
independent renewal beyond the initial lease while COMMIT is blocked and the
queue is full, and a backend terminated during COMMIT returning the original
typed unknown outcome. The private-cluster script runs these witnesses. Their raw database fault
fixtures live under `store/`, inside the adapter test boundary; the queue
module retains only shared-port tests.

**Fix round 2 qualification completed (2026-10-23):** explicit cross-thread
request attribution repaired the earlier stopped checkpoint. Both unchanged RT0
tables matched all twelve warm rows, the adoption witnesses and full 29-case
lifecycle target passed, and every requested suite ran. The lane report retains
the reproducible unrelated full-CLI/device failures and evaluator Serializable
stress failure; this is scoped qualification, not a clean-full-suite claim.
K0-Q landed at `901a362705`; the third review's two P2s and four low-priority
findings are repaired by this fix-forward, with fresh checks recorded in the
JOURNAL.

The queue, concurrent HTTP, overload, full-suite and local W4 evidence are in
[the lane report](../snapback4/reports/20261022-k0q/REPORT.md). The SQLite
flush-per-member ceiling remains; batching is the next slice.

### 3.5 Each command keeps its own place

Folding statements into fewer flights must not change what each one sees
or locks:

- **The owner fence is its own command and comes first.** It is the first
  lock of every writing transaction (parent §5.1). A single statement that
  joins `_owner` and `_head` can let the planner take the head lock first,
  which parent §5.1 changed because that order deadlocks with takeover.
- **The receipt re-check is its own command, after the head lock,** for
  the same reason as the generation check below: the receipt a concurrent
  copy committed while this transaction waited for the lock is visible only
  to a later command.
- **The generation check is its own command, after the head lock.**
  Writers run READ COMMITTED (`store.rs:98–101`). Adoption locks `_head`
  and then publishes `_backend.generation` (`opening.rs:105–125`). A
  statement that both locks `_head` and reads `_backend` takes its
  snapshot before it waits for the lock, so it can miss an adoption it
  waited on. A separate later command takes a new snapshot and sees it.
- **A failed fence stops everything after it on the server.** A
  `SELECT … FOR KEY SHARE` that returns no row raises nothing. The fence
  takes the key-share lock and then raises explicitly on a token mismatch,
  for example through an attach-installed function. The commands after it
  are locks and reads, never writes, so an aborted flight leaves nothing
  behind.
- **Every check that decides whether to commit happens before COMMIT is
  sent.** Some are statement errors the server raises: the fence, unique
  and check constraints, deferred-rule failures. Others are results the
  client inspects: the tail's committed-row count must be exactly one
  (`tail.rs:62`, `:80`), released savepoints and member dispositions must
  match the frame, and the program's outcome must be a commit. A flight
  that folds any of these either makes the server raise on it, or ends at
  that check and lets the client inspect the result before the next
  flight. The count includes that wait. Fault tests cover a zero-row tail,
  a statement failure inside each folded flight, and an ambiguous COMMIT.
- **COMMIT is its own flight.** Today a failure before COMMIT is sent is a
  definite abort (`ownership.rs:129–131`). If COMMIT shared a message with
  earlier statements, a lost message would be outcome-unknown even when
  the server never saw COMMIT. Folding COMMIT needs that wider window
  accepted in the parent's retry table first (§5.2), and this RFC does not
  propose it.

## 13. G4a read refinement (2026-10-23)

The accepted controls design's G4a is commissioned in lane `sb4-pg/g4a`.
Short HTTP admission now has 30 executing workers and 98 bounded waiting
slots; queued work owns no database connection. The original absolute body
deadline and intake age survive the queue; expiry and overflow return the
existing non-draining rate-limit refusal. The matched reader/total database
caps remain 30/32. This replaces the root's historical no-extra-queue note
for short requests only. Burst/expiry witnesses and unchanged RT0 tables are
recorded in [the G4a report](../snapback4/reports/20261023-g4a-reads/REPORT.md).

G4a resolves open question 1 for `/q`'s **bounded POST body**: its ingress
uses only parsed/carried facts, with no database call or authorization.
Ordinary capability hints are bounded to 1,024 entries per server/store and
never grant authority or authenticate metadata. Cold credentials use the
existing discovery statement after body intake, then the fresh query
snapshot; action/custom resolution stays uncached. The final shared Rust
resolver compares principal, kind/scope and store binding. Warm W1/W2 fall
to 2/1 PG flights (SQLite 5/5 statements); unknown tokens retain one PG
flight. Existing authority witnesses and both lowered ratchets pass; the
report records cold discovery and the separate added carried-hint witnesses.
This narrowly replaces §7's no-cross-request-facts rule for non-authorizing
ordinary hints; fresh final authority remains mandatory.

G4a's distinct W3-shaped RT0 baseline, after ingress refinement and before
fact composition, is 134 warm PG flights / 132 SQLite statements (138/132
first use). The 32 membership/room-total/unread shape includes an excluded
33rd room; it retains the original W1/W2 and mutation classes. It does not
reuse the M1 harness's different census. Both ratchets pass.

The bounded physical collector subsequently reduces warm W1 and the new W3
class to **one PG flight each**, alongside W2 at one. It fetches ordinary
authority/identity/head/generation and the entire admitted read closure in
one SELECT; the ordinary Rust resolver/interpreter still decides rules,
arithmetic, refusals, meters and cursors. The shared exact cache replays the
same logical read charges and prevents redundant prefetch. Physical admission
reuses the 4 MiB prefetch envelope with 16 access nodes and 32 prepared shapes;
unsupported or unproved forms retain ordinary access. Identity-dependent SQL
address extraction declines JSON/asset-bearing sources and NUL enums, keeping
valid unrelated JSON NUL on the portable Rust decoder. This is access batching,
not B3, a result cache or an authority grant. Both-store meter/outcome and
planted-fact oracles, coherent-cut/revocation and NUL witnesses are in the
G4a report; full qualification is recorded there separately.

The serving statement-fault witness covers every BEGIN/facts/ROLLBACK
boundary of composed reads and retains the original sequence boundaries
using a genuine conservative lifetime-table fallback. Each injected error
must preserve the complete store census and prevent the later-command
sentinel. The 19-test literal/serving suite passes after adapting its stale
expectation of W1's removed second flight.


G4a fix round 1 (2026-10-24), rebased onto RT6 fix round 2, distinguishes
point accesses from ranges even for empty `.id` prefixes. A unique primary
point retains a separate exact-prefix fact for metered lookup; neither fact
can satisfy a wider page. Unknown nodes, incomplete keys/images, malformed
row payloads and malformed hex return the ordinary fallback without indexing
unchecked vectors. Dependent fields with defaults or optional types decline
composition before execution, retaining Rust's sparse-image materialization.

Before SQL parses an address source, a conservative text check for the
canonical NUL escape declines that image and the whole speculative payload.
It preserves stored bytes, including escaped-backslash spellings that merely
cause conservative fallback. Fixed NUL keys decline at admission. Current
text ingress already rejects control U+0000; the new oracle specifically
covers retained canonical text images which ordinary PostgreSQL and SQLite
can read. Any speculative statement failure rolls back its transaction,
discards every speculative fact, and resolves authority/rows in a fresh
ordinary snapshot. A real SQL-error witness revokes the principal between
rollback and reopening, then requires `E_AUTH` rather than old authority or
a speculative SQL error. Existing read-fault boundaries now demand complete
ordinary outcomes after failed composition; sequence/writer errors retain
their original refusal and no-later-command checks.

A separate timer expires queued short requests while every request worker
is blocked, using the earlier original intake/body deadline. It owns no DB
reader. One worker's exit preserves the queue for healthy workers; loss of
all workers refuses it immediately. The 98 queued sockets are added to the
existing 128 non-wait HTTP connection reservation, so small long-wait
configurations retain mutation room. Database caps and the 30/98 execution/
queue limits remain unchanged. The Postgres script explicitly runs ignored
closure oracles and the commit/revocation cut; its evaluator uses a separate
Cargo target namespace instead of deleting shared build artifacts. Both RT0
tables remain byte-identical to the commissioned G4a baseline.

The public Postgres witness uses the commissioned 22 GiB disk stop floor,
matching the lane monitor, instead of its former unrelated 40 GiB constant.

Round-1 qualification passes build, full core and binary, both unchanged RT0
ratchets, the complete Postgres script (1,184.70 s, including ignored closure
oracles/revocation and public serving) and root ref-check. Both private PG16
masters are stopped and independently absent; no owned processes remain.
Detailed before/after evidence is retained in the commissioned external
`rt6g4a` directory; the append-only journal records counts and cleanup.

    59	    Complete,
    60	    Abort,
    61	    Failed,
    62	}

    74	    pub(crate) fn complete<T>(self, value: T) -> MemberResult<T> {

    78	            end: Frame::Complete,
    79	            value,
    80	        })

    83	    /// Explicit rollback keeps its fallible contract, including replay paths.

    98	    fn rollback_frame(&self) -> Result<(), RuntimeError> {

   106	            self.frame.set(Frame::Abort);
